## Document 009: 115年10月海關進出口貿易初步統計 (2026-11-09)

115 年 10 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
10 月出口 967.6 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 10.9%，較上
年同月增 58.9%(按新臺幣計算增 66.6%)；累 計 1 至 10 月出口 7,582.9
億美元，較上年同期增 47.7%(按新臺幣計算增 51.1%)。 
（二）進口 
10 月進口 688.9 億美元，亦創歷年單月新高，較上月增 8.3%，較上
年同月增 75.1%(按新臺幣計算增 83.5%)；累 計 1 至 10 月進口 5,697.6
億美元，較上年同期增 44.6%(按新臺幣計算增 47.6%)。 
（三）出入超 
10 月出超 278.8 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 63.3 億美元；
累計 1 至 10 月出超 1,885.4 億美元，較上年同期增 689.7 億美元。

二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
受惠於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及雲端服務等應用商機持續拓展，
加上適逢傳統外銷旺季，以及價格攀升效應，10 月出口較上年同月
增 358.7 億美元(+58.9%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 資通與視聽產品出口 476.0 億美元(歷年單月新高)，較上年同月增
200.7 億美元(+72.9%)，其中電腦及其附屬單元增 151.6 億美元
(+67.5%)、電腦之零附件增 18.5 億美元(+1.0 倍)、儲存媒體增 17.3
億美元(+2.1 倍)、交換器及路由器增 11.7 億美元(+1.3 倍)。 
2. 電子零組件出口 326.8 億美元(歷年單月新高)，較上年同月增 124.3
億美元(+61.4%)，其中積體電路增 120.5 億美元(+63.0%)、印刷電
路增 1.9 億美元(+38.5%)、電容器及電阻器增 1.2 億美元(+48.6%)。 
3. 基本金屬及其製品出口 28.8 億美元，較上年同月增 7.6 億美元
(+35.7%)，其中銅及其製品增 4.7 億美元(+1.2 倍)、鋼鐵及其製品
增 1.9 億美元(+16.4%)。 
4. 機械出口 28.8 億美元(歷年單月新高)，較上年同月增 7.5 億美元
(+35.5%)，其中生產半導體等機械增 2.9 億美元(+57.5%)、機器、
工廠或實驗室設備增 1.4 億美元(+70.0%)。 
5. 礦產品出口 15.5 億美元，較上年同月增 6.5 億美元(+71.6%)，其
中石油煉製品增 5.7 億美元(+72.2%)。

（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 10 月出口較上年同期增 2,448.3 億美元(+47.7%)，以資通
與視聽產品增 1,423.3 億美元(+72.5%)、電子零組件增 850.5 億美元
(+47.5%)增加較多，礦產品、機械亦各增 41.9 億美元(+36.8%)、41.5
億美元(+19.8%)。

生產等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
隨 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求之帶動，積體電路、資通產品
及半導體設備進口持續強勁，加上國際原物料行情居高，10 月總進
口較上年同月增 295.5 億美元(+75.1%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口 458.3 億美元，較上年同月增 195.0 億美元(+74.0%)，
其中電子零組件增 136.1 億美元(+1.1 倍)、礦產品增 22.8 億美元
(+58.0%)、電腦之零附件增 16.2 億美元(+70.0%)、基本金屬增 6.2
億美元(+33.4%)、塑化原料增 5.0 億美元(+22.0%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 163.4 億美元，較上年同月增 84.1 億美元(+1.1 倍)，
其中資通設備增 46.9 億美元(+2.0 倍)、半導體設備增 22.4 億美元
(+83.4%)、交通運輸設備增 7.6 億美元(+7.0 倍)。 
3. 消費品進口 61.1 億美元，較上年同月增 18.8 億美元(+44.5%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增 16.0 億美元(+5.3 倍)、食品增 0.5 億
美元(+5.1%)、小客車增 0.4 億美元(+10.2%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
10 月對美國、歐洲、日本出口創新高 
1. 對美國出口 329.3 億美元，較上年同月增 118.1 億美元(+55.9%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 98.3 億美元(+56.6%)、電子零組件增 13.5
億美元(+1.7 倍)、機械增 2.4 億美元(+41.6%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 204.4 億美元，較上年同月增 61.4 億美元
(+42.9%)，其中電子零組件增 47.4 億美元(+48.0%)、資通與視聽
產品增 5.6 億美元(+36.5%)、基本金屬及其製品增 3.2 億美元
(+95.2%)。 
3. 對東協出口 184.6 億美元，較上年同月增 84.1 億美元(+83.8%)，
其中電子零組件增 38.6 億美元(+75.5%)、資通與視聽產品增 36.5
億美元(+1.4 倍)、礦產品增 5.0 億美元(+2.4 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 59.5 億美元，較上年同月增 18.7 億美元(+46.0%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 10.7 億美元(+47.9%)、電子零組件增 3.3 億
美元(+81.3%)、機械增 1.2 億美元(+50.8%)。 
5. 對日本出口 36.7 億美元，較上年同月增 12.2 億美元(+49.6%)，其
中電子零組件增 7.5 億美元(+63.2%)、資通與視聽產品增 3.2 億美
元(+1.3 倍)、化學品增 0.5 億美元(+47.0%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 10 月對美國出口 2,407.1 億美元，較上年同期增 891.1 億美
元(+58.8%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,805.3 億美元，增 416.6 億美
元(+30.0%)，對東協出口 1,524.1 億美元，增 547.6 億美元(+56.1%)，
對歐洲出口 481.3 億美元，增 157.1 億美元(+48.5%)，對日本出口 314.4
億美元，增 70.5 億美元(+28.9%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
10 月自東協進口 134.1 億美元，較上年同月增 82.3 億美元(+1.6 倍)，
自南韓進口 130.0 億 美 元，增 73.1 億美元(+1.3 倍)，自中國大陸與香
港進口 128.3 億美元，增 51.9 億美元(+67.9%)，自日本進口 62.0 億
美元，增 18.8 億美元(+43.4%)，自美國進口 53.6 億美元，增 14.7 億
美元(+37.9%)，自歐洲進口 48.2 億美元，增 9.5 億美元(+24.6%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 10 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較，自東協增 85.6%、
自南韓增 72.7%、自中國大陸與香港增 45.3%、自日本增 27.4%、自
美國增 23.0%、自歐洲增 12.1%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）今年 1-9 月主要國家/地區出口均呈上升，其中南韓(1-10 月)、香港、
新加坡受 AI 相關需求帶動科技供應鏈及區域貿易活動熱絡，累計出
口分別年增 60.6%、52.3%、41.3%；中國大陸、美國各年增 21.3%
及 14.3%，德國(1-8 月)、日本各增 8.8%、8.3%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步成長，累計 1-9 月以香港進口年增 51.8%較
高，新加坡、中國大陸、南 韓 (1-10 月)分別年增 39.0%、27.5%及 21.4%，
德國(1-8 月)、日本、美國各增 8.4%、8.2%、6.7%。

### Page 7

7
圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）隨全球人工智慧(AI)基礎建設投資強勁成長，帶動相關供應鏈需求擴
增，加以外銷產品價格攀升，10 月出口達 967.6 億美元，首度突破
900 億美元，年增 58.9%，連續 36 個月正成長；進口 688.9 億美元，
同為歷年單月最高，年增 75.1%，主要受惠於 AI 產業之國際分工與
出口引申需求增加，以及國際原物料行情高漲；出超 278.8 億美元亦
創新高。累計 1 至 10 月出口年增 47.7%、進口增 44.6%。
（二）10 月出口主要貨品中，資通與視聽產品、電子零組件出口值雙創單月
新高，各年增 72.9%及 61.4%，主因 AI、高效能運算及雲端服務等應
用商機熱絡；其餘貨類出口平均增 25.7%，其中礦產品因油價支撐增
71.6%，機械、電機產品受惠於半導體業者擴充產能及 AI 需求外溢，
增幅皆達 3 成，基本金屬及其製品亦增 35.7%。累 計 1 至 10 月資通與
視聽產品、電子零組件合占總出口比重約 8 成，年增 60.6%。

（三）10 月對主要市場出口年增率均逾 4 成，以對東協增 83.8%較突出，
對美國、日本、歐洲出口規模皆為單月新高，各增 55.9%、49.6%及
46.0%，對中國大陸與香港增 42.9%。累 計 1 至 10 月對五大市場出口
增幅介於 28%~59%，其中對美國出口年增 58.8%、對東協增 56.1%，
占總出口比重各為 31.7%及 20.1%，分別寫下近 36 年同期及歷年同
期新高紀錄。
（四）展望未來，全球經濟仍面臨地緣政治衝突、美國貿易政策走向等不
確定因素，惟隨人工智慧應用逐漸擴散至推論、代理式 AI，且國際
雲端服務業者持續布建 AI 基礎設施，帶動半導體、伺服器等相關供
應鏈需求延續，加以科技新品相繼推 出、年底消費旺季來臨之挹注，
我國第 4 季出口可望維持強勁成長態勢。

Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line
In this report, we focus on preliminary JMTBA orders, which we
believe could be a catalyst for AirTAC's and Hiwin's share prices.
According to the Japan Machine Tool Builders' Association (JMTBA), headline

orders dropped 8% MoM but grew 43% YoY in October  2026 (vs. +13% MoM/
+60% YoY in September 2026), to ¥204bn.
• Domestic orders were -18% MoM/+47% YoY (vs. +24% MoM/+46% YoY in
September), at ¥52bn.
• Overseas orders were -5% MoM/+41% YoY (vs. +9% MoM/+67% YoY  in
September), at ¥152bn.
Our view:
• For overseas orders, the growth remains solid on a YoY basis. We think the
sequential decline is likely from China as its manufacturing PMI edged down

to 49.3 in Oct (vs. 50.1 in Sep). Meanwhile, North America and Europe could
prove more resilient, with manufacturing PMIs staying relatively firm at
55.5/52.7 in Oct (vs. 54.5/52.9 in Sep)
• We remain OW on both Airtac (1590.TW) and Hiwin (2049.TW), as we see
company-specific drivers on top of the industry recovery trend:
° Airtac should continue to benefit from its own market share gains.
Valuation also appears undemanding at 15x 2027e P/E (vs. 25x average

since 2020).
° Hiwin should  see margin expansion in the coming quarters, driven by
higher utilization and price hikes. Current valuation of 27x 2027 P/E
implies upside vs. peak cycle valuation at 35-40x P/E.
Morgan Stanley does and seeks to do business with
companies covered in Morgan Stanley Research. As a result,
investors should be aware that the firm may have a conflict of
interest that could affect the objectivity of Morgan Stanley
Research. Investors should consider Morgan Stanley

communications with a subject company, public appearances
and trading securities held by a research analyst account.

Morgan Stanley Research 3
Valuation Methodology and Risks
AirTAC International (1590.TW)
Base case, 25x 2027e P/E. We believe this methodology better captures earnings growth
momentum for industrial automation component plays (market sentiment moves along
with business cycles). We think our valuation of 25x P/E is reasonable (vs. average of 25x since
2020).
Risks to Upside
n Stronger macroeconomic climate in China
n Stronger-than-expected demand from industrial automation

Risks to Downside
n Sharper economic downturn in China
n Miniature linear guideway business development taking more time
n Larger-than-expected investment in new products but weaker-than-expected demand
Hiwin Technologies Corp. (2049.TW)
Base case, P/E. We think this valuation methodology better captures Hiwin's earnings growth
momentum, as its valuation tends to move with the business cycle. We apply a target multiple
of 34x to our 2027 EPS estimate. We view this multiple as justified by the 50% operating profit

CAGR we project for 2025-28.
Risks to Upside
n Stronger industrial automation demand pickup
n Market share gains
n Earlier-than-expected revenue contribution from humanoids
Risks to Downside
n Weaker industrial automation demand
n More pricing pressure
n Market share loss
n More broad-based impact on the global economy from geopolitical risks

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views

below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

## Document 206: [000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다실적에 수급을 더한다 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다SK하이닉스(000660) 실적에 수급을 더한다실적에 수급을 더한다 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

매수 (유지)  
여전히 가격 상승 구간 
SK하이닉스의 26년 3분기 매출액은 95.8조원, 영업이익 75.1조원으로 예상한다. 
이전 전망 대비 감소한 수준이다. 원/달러 환율 영향이다. 영업이익률은 78.4%로 전 
분기 대비 2.1%p 상승할 전망이다. DRAM 매출액은 73.1조원, 영업이익 
59.2조원으로 추정한다. DRAM ASP는 이전 전망 대비 상향 조정했다. 일부 제품 
가격 인상을 반영한 영향이다. Bit Growth는 이전 전망 수준을 유지했다. ASP 
상향이 수익성 개선에 미친 영향은 크지 않을 전망이다. NAND 매출액은 22.4조원, 
영업이익은 15.9조원으로 예상한다. 
Global Top 수준 주주환원 규모/자사주 살 게 더 있다 
SK하이닉스는 2026년 8월 19일 주주환원에 대한 새로운 정책을 제시했다. FCF에서 
CAPEX를 제외한 금액의 50% 이상을 주주환원에 사용한다는게 핵심이다. 2026년 
기준으로 최소 90조원 수준일 것으로 추정한다. 자사주 중심으로 주주환원을 계획 
중이고 이미 집행 중인 40조원 이외에 4분기 중에 추가로 자사주 매입이 진행될 
것으로 예상한다. 배당도 일부 집행이 되겠지만 자사주가 중심이 될 전망이다. 
흔들리지 말아야 할 이유는 많다 
SK하이닉스에 대한 투자의견은 매수를 유지한다. AI 모델의 성장으로 메모리에 
대한 수요가 지속적으로 높아지고 있고, 이에 비해 공급 증가는 제한적인 상황이 
상당기간 지속될 것으로 전망하기 때문이다. 이로 인해 현재 수준의 DRAM, NAND 
가격에 변동성은 낮고, 높은 수익성이 유지되면서 안정적 현금흐름 창출이 가능할 
것으로 전망한다. 현재 주가는 지나치게 저평가되어 있는 국면에 있다고 판단한다. 
목표주가는 12개월 예상 EPS 446,092원에 PER 9배를 적용한 4,000,000원을 
유지한다.

Compliance Notice 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 외부의 압력이나 부당한 간섭없이 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하여 작성되었음을 확인합니다. 
동 자료는 기관투자가 또는 제3자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다. 
동 자료는 조사분석자료 작성에 참여한 외부인(계열회사 및 그 임직원등)이 없습니다. 
조사분석 담당자 및 배우자는 해당종목과 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 
동 자료에 언급된 종목의 지분율 1%이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
당사는 상기 명시한 사항 외 고지해야 하는 특별한 이해관계가 없습니다.

종목명 담당자 담당자(배우자) 보유여부 1%이상 보유여부 유가증권 발행관련 계열사 관계여부 공개매수 사무취급 IPO 회사채 지급보증 중대한 이해관계 M&A 관련 수량 취득가 취득일 
해당 사항 없음

## Document 212: [005380] 현대차(005380) 4분기 반등 기대현대차(005380) 4분기 반등 기대4분기 반등 기대 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[005380] 현대차(005380) 4분기 반등 기대현대차(005380) 4분기 반등 기대4분기 반등 기대 - 기업.pdf

### Page 1

현대차 (005380)

EARNINGS PREVIEW Company Analysis | 자동차/로봇 | 2026.10.16  
4분기 반등 기대

3Q26 Preview 
현대차는 3Q26E 매출액 42.4조원(-9.4% YoY, -13.9% QoQ), 영업이익 2.5조원(-
2.8% YoY, -13.5% QoQ, OPM 5.8%)을 기록, 컨센서스를 하회할 전망이다. 각 부문
별 영업이익은 자동차 1.7조원(-2.0% YoY, -14.9% QoQ, OPM 4.8%), 금융 5,501억
원(-4.5% YoY, -27.5% QoQ, OPM 6.9%)으로 예상한다. 
생산 정상화 및 신차 확대를 통한 수익성 개선 기대 
현대차는 글로벌 도매 판매 91.8만대(-11.5% 이하 YoY), 중국 제외 판매량은 90.9
만대(-9.6%)를 기록했다. 7~8월에 발생한 파업 영향으로 약 5.5만대 규모의 생산 차
질이 발생했다. 파업 영향으로 HEV 판매 증가율이 1%에 그치면서, HEV 판매 확대에 
따른 믹스 개선 효과도 시장 기대에 미치지 못했다.  
4분기부터는 3분기 파업 영향으로 지연된 생산량 회복 및 신차 확대가 본격화되면서, 
전분기 대비 매출 및 수익성 개선이 기대된다. 동사는 보급형 전기차 아이오닉3의 튀
르키예 생산을 9월부터 시작했으며, 이를 통해 유럽 시장 판매량은 점차 회복될 전망
이다. 또한, 1) HMGMA를 통한 HEV 미국 현지 생산 확대, 2) 제네시스 HEV 모델 순
차적 출시를 통해 내년부터 북미를 중심으로 믹스 개선이 본격화될 전망이다. 
투자의견은 Buy를 유지하고 목표주가는 520,000원으로 하향 
2027년 영업이익은 11.2조원(+10.2%)를 기록할 것으로 예상된다. 원화 약세 지속을 
반영해 이익 추정치를 하향하며, 이에 따라 목표주가를 하향한다. 다만, 현재 주가는 
12M Fwd P/E 기준 6.1배로 Legacy OEM 평균(7.7배)보다 낮은 수준이다. 1) RMAC 
성과 구체화, 2) RA(로봇 생산법인) 착공 등 Physical AI 사업이 구체화될 경우 밸류에
이션 할증이 가능할 전망이다. 
 Analyst 이병근 
bglee@ls-sec.co.kr 
Buy (유지)

주가 추이

3Q 큰 폭의 판매 감소, 매출/영업이익 시장 기대치 하회 예상 
3Q26 동사 매출액과 영업이익은 각각 44.6조원(-4.5% 이하 YoY)과 2.4조원
(-5.4%) 기록하며 시장 기대치 하회 전망. 3Q 매출액은 글로벌 판매 부진 지속
과 환율 하락, 그리고 친환경차 판매 성장 둔화 등의 영향에 기인. 특히, 3Q 판
매량은 918.7만대(-11.5% 이하 YoY)로 전년 동기 대비 큰 폭 감소하며 매출
액에 부정적 영향. 이는 7월과 8월 발생한 파업 영향으로 약 5.5만대 규모의 생
산 물량 차질이 발생한 부분에 기인. 파업은 기존 물량/믹스 개선에 기여했던 
글로벌 HEV 판매도 둔화(+3.4%)시키면서 매출에 부정적 영향을 심화. 
3Q 수익성은 4~5월 상승했던 원자재 투입에 따른 원가 상승 영향과 친환경차 
판매 감소에 따른 부정적 믹스 영향 불가피. 이에 시장 기대치인 3.0조원을 크
게 하회할 전망. 다만, 기말 환율이 1,356원/$ 수준으로 낮아지면서 판매보증비 
외화환산손익 영향은 다소 개선(0.4%p) 예상되며, 관세 영향 또한 전년 동기 
대비 약 1조원(2.1%p) 감소하면서 수익성 악화 영향을 일부 만회할 전망.  
’27년 지연된 신차효과 가시화로 반등 여력 충분 
4Q부터는 3Q 파업 영향 등으로 지연된 신차 확대 본격화되고, 현지 수요 높은 
차종 중심의 물량 확대를 통해 매출 회복 및 이익 성장 가시화가 기대. 아반떼
와 투싼 FMC 확대로 국내 및 해외 판매 둔화가 점진적 만회되고, 지난 9월부
터 터키에서 아이오닉3 생산이 본격화되면서 유럽에서의 시장 부진 회복이 기
대. 또한, GV80과 G80 HEV 모델 출시로 내년부터 국내/북미 중심 판매 확대
되며 올해 부진했던 물량/믹스 개선에 기여할 것으로 기대. ’27년 HMGMA에
서 HEV 신차 생산 투입 예정되어 있어 상대적으로 높은 수요가 지속되는 북미
에서의 HEV 현지 생산 확대로 가동률 증가와 관세 부담 완화도 수익성 개선에 
기여할 것으로 기대. 
부정적 환율 고려 필요하나 ’27년 강한 증익 기대  
’27년 동사 영업이익 추정치는 12.4조원(+22.3%)으로 ’26년 감익에서 ’27년 
증익 전환 예상. 최근 환율 추세 고려 시 내년 상반기까지 부정적인 환율 영향 
확대 가능성 존재하나, 기존 노후 차종에서 현지 수요 대응 가능한 신차 중심 
생산/판매 확대로 물량/믹스 개선되며 부정적 환율 영향 상쇄 기대.  
12M EPS 추정치 하향(-11.8%)과 상반기 적용한 코스피 프리미엄 제거(-
10%)에 따라 목표주가를 670,000원으로 하향. 그러나, 연 내 RMAC 운영 
성과와 RA 설립, 12월 모셔널 자율주행 상용화와 웨이모向 로보택시 
양산/공급 등 미래 사업에서의 가시성도 여전히 기대 가능한 부분.

[ Compliance Notice ] (공표일: 2026 년 10 월 8 일) 
이 자료는 조사분석 담당자가 객관적 사실에 근거해 작성하였으며, 타인의 부당한 압력이나 간섭없이 본인의 견을 정확하게 
반영했습니다. 본인은 이 자료에서 다룬 종목과 관련해 공표일 현재 관련 법규상 알려야 할 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 본인은 이 
자료를 기관투자자 또는 제 3 자에게 사전에 제공한 사실이 없습니다. (김성래) 
저희 회사는 공표일 현재 이 자료에서 다룬 종목의 발행주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
이 자료는 투자자의 증권투자를 돕기 위해 당사 고객에 한하여 배포되는 자료로서 저작권이 당사에 있으며 불법 복제 및 배포를 금
합니다. 이 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료나 정보출처로부터 얻은 것이지만, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완
전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 이 자료는 어떠한 경우에도 고객의 증권투자 결과와 관련된 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙으로 사
용될 수 없습니다.

### Page 8

현대차 (005380) [한화리서치]

8 
[ 종목 투자등급 ] 
당사는 개별 종목에 대해 향후 1 년간 +15% 이상의 절대수익률이 기대되는 종목에 대해 Buy(매수) 의견을 제시합니다. 또한 절대수
익률 -15~+15%가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Hold(보유) 의견을, -15% 이하가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Sell(매도) 의견을 제시합니다. 밸류
에이션 방법 등 절대수익률 산정은 개별 종목을 커버하는 애널리스트의 추정에 따르며, 목표주가 산정이나 투자의견 변경 주기는 종
목별로 다릅니다.

[ 산업 투자의견 ] 
당사는 산업에 대해 향후 1 년간 해당 업종의 수익률이 과거 수익률에 비해 양호한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Positive(긍정
적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 또한 향후 1 년간 수익률이 과거 수익률과 유사한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Neutral(중립적) 
의견을, 과거 수익률보다 부진한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Negative(부정적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 산업별 수익률 전
망은 해당 산업 내 분석대상 종목들에 대한 담당 애널리스트의 분석과 판단에 따릅니다.

[ 당사 조사분석자료의 투자등급 부여 비중 ] (기준일: 2026 년 9 월 30 일)

## Document 298: CNB Minutes_ Neither hikes nor cuts in the pipeline

Source PDF: all report pdf/CNB Minutes_ Neither hikes nor cuts in the pipeline.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-11-13
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The Bank Board increased interest rates to 4% in a close call,
and we see valid arguments for both positions. Second-round
eﬀects will push inﬂation above the upper bound of the target
corridor for some time, while dwindling economic activity
warrants lots of caution. We see neither hikes nor cuts as the
most likely outcome for quite some time
David Havrlant
The Czech National Bank's decision to raise rates on 5 November was a close
call
Assessing the impact of Hormuz

The Czech National Bank increased the policy rate by 25bp to 4% in
a split vote on 5 November, with Governor Ales Michl and board
members Jan Kubicek, Jan Prochazka and Jakub Seidler voting for
a hike. Meanwhile, Deputy Governors Eva Zamrazilova and Jan Frait,
along with board member Karina Kubelkova, favoured interest rate
stability. We assess the current situation and the risks to the
outlook as complex and see relevant justiﬁcation for both options,

depending on whether your concerns are more on the side of

### Page 2

second-round inﬂationary eﬀects or on the side of disappointing
economic activity and the consequences for consumption.
The Bank Board assessed overall risks as pro-inﬂationary. There
was an agreement that the secondary impacts of the energy shock
have not been signiﬁcantly reﬂected in domestic inﬂation so far.
However, as the Hormuz conﬂict becomes more protracted, the
likelihood of tangible second-round eﬀects increases. Jakub Seidler
noted that global energy prices have been elevated for too long, so

it would be diﬃcult to avoid a pass-through to other segments of
consumer prices. Deputy Governor Zamrazilova emphasised that
much depends on further developments in the Middle Eastern
conﬂict. Should tensions ease in the near future, a rate hike at the
November meeting would not be necessary. The key question is
whether the conﬂict will eventually pass or become the new
normal, with the latter implying persistently high energy costs.
Deputy Governor Frait stated that energy prices remaining at these

levels for such an extended period could have a negative impact on
demand and economic activity, mainly by reducing the real
incomes of households and companies. The current negative
supply shock has hit the Czech economy from a favourable starting
position, but the factors that helped keep inﬂation close to the
target this year are about to fade. The increase in longer-term rates
contributes to the restrictive stance of monetary policy, and implies

some softening in domestic lending activity. The board members
agreed that this is a key factor reducing the need for further
tightening for the time being.
To rise or not to rise?
However, as Jan Kubicek noted, core inﬂation is not slowing down
as previously assumed and is even slightly increasing. This
represents a fundamental concern, especially in combination with
the disappointing real growth performance. That said, producer
prices have embarked on an upward trend and may put further

pressure on the core rate. Jakub Seidler and Karina Kubelkova
emphasised that the renewed price increases in the energy and
food segment are usually the key drivers when it comes to inﬂation

### Page 3

expectations, and the ﬁrst signs of an increase are already visible.
And we all know that unanchored inﬂation expectations are one of
the things that keep central bankers awake at night.
Producer prices will drive consumer prices
Source: CZSO, ING, Macrobond
En gros, among the main reasons for a rate hike in November were:
i) persistent core inﬂation, ii) still robust wage dynamics and iii)
second-round eﬀects expected to bite soon. Among the main

reasons for staying on hold and evaluating were: i) secondary
inﬂationary impacts are so far limited, ii) long-term rates deliver
restriction, iii) disappointing economic activity.
We see the arguments in both directions as valid, which made the
November decision relatively complex and was reﬂected in the
close vote. Time will tell whether the second-round eﬀects that we
believe are just kicking in will prove signiﬁcant without weighing too
heavily on economic activity. Or whether the deteriorating real

purchasing power and dwindling real wage growth could lead to
weaker household consumption, which would in turn put
downward pressure on inﬂation. Both eﬀects are certainly
interconnected, which only underscores the beauty of
macroeconomics, and the formidable power of prices to clear the
market.

### Page 4

Punchy inﬂation across price segments
When it comes to our inﬂation forecast, we deﬁnitely see pro-
inﬂationary pressures when looking ahead. We expect annual
producer price growth to peak at around 7% in industry early next
year and at around 12% in agriculture mid-next year. At the same
time, the annual pace of import prices already quickened to above
8% in September. Those are not low growth rates, and all are going
to feed into consumer prices sooner or later. At the same time, the

koruna continues to weaken against the dollar, making imports
pricier. With all that on board, we see the yearly average of inﬂation
picking up from 2.1% this year to 3.2% next, reaching a peak of just
below 4% in February.
Inﬂation to hover above the target for
longer
Source: CNB, ING, Macrobond

distributors and factor in a 3.5% increase in electricity prices, a

6.5% increase in natural gas prices, an 8% increase in heating
prices and a 6% rise in water charges. Nonetheless, not all
distributors will apply the same increase and not all households will
be aﬀected equally. Based on our best estimate, we expect a 3%
monthly increase in regulated prices in January, resulting in a 3.8%

Food prices are set to shift from an anti-inﬂationary position this

Neither hikes nor cuts in the pipeline
When it comes to core inﬂation, two opposing forces are set for a
duel: second-round eﬀects, coupled with rising import prices,
against a softening housing market and crumbling real purchasing

power. Timing is of the essence. We expect the former to strike ﬁrst,
but the latter to respond without much hesitation. For now, we see
the forces as roughly balanced, keeping average core inﬂation just
below 3% next year.

Consumption could slow tangibly along with
real wages
Source: CZSO, ING, Macrobond
So yes, upbeat inﬂation across price segments warrants restrictive
monetary policy. Meanwhile, weakening economic activity
warrants a lot of caution when it comes to any future rate hikes.
The thing is, we see real GDP growth averaging just 1.7% this year
and lifting oﬀ only gradually to 2.1% next year. Meanwhile, we see
household consumption slowing tangibly to 1.8% next year from

2.5% this year. Indeed, we are likely positioned at the lower end of
the forecast range, yet we take the view that a slowdown in real
wage growth will eventually take its toll. Therefore, we see neither
further hikes nor cuts when looking way ahead, with the 4% policy
rate settling in as the new normal.

GIS-KY(6456 TT)       法說/座談摘要 
雷射封裝業務進展順利，2Q27有望顯著放量 
⚫ 公司積極轉型自傳統觸控模組轉向半導體光學 
⚫ 雷射封測試量產進行中，預期年底出貨，2027將有顯著貢獻 
結論：GIS-KY 3Q26營收有起色，但仍受終端市場需求不振及客戶端記
憶體缺貨的影響，但整體財務結構與淨現金部位仍穩健。公司目前正積
極推動業務轉型，從傳統觸控模組轉向半導體光學領域，並已擴大資本
支出佈局光通訊雷射光源封測與 AR/VR等新產品，預計這些新事業將於
2027至 2028年成為公司顯著的營收成長動能。

3Q26財務數據 
⚫ 營收 144億元(QoQ+37.6%，YoY-10.5%)，主要受到終端市場需求
不振及客戶端記憶體缺貨的影響。 
⚫ 毛利率 4.02%(QoQ+0.68ppts，YoY-2.46ppts) 
⚫ 營業費用 12.11億元，前三季單季皆維持在 11.2億元左右的水準。
因公司處於轉型期，於 R&D的投資相對較多。 
⚫ 營業利益-6.3億元 
⚫ 稅前淨利-6.69億元 
⚫ 單季 EPS -3.15元 
⚫ 稅後淨利-7.25億元 
⚫ 單季 EPS -2.17元。 
產品營收占比 
⚫ 3Q26：平板佔 40%、筆電佔 28%、指紋辨識模組佔 13%、其他佔
19%。 
⚫ 1Q-3Q26：平板佔 32%、筆電佔 21%、指紋辨識模組佔 9%、其他
佔 38%。 
法說會內容摘要 
1.  公司中長期轉型策略：半導體光學(Semi Optics) 
• 光學元件：主要應用於 AR/VR眼鏡、Waveguide及抬頭顯示器
（HUD），涵蓋奈米壓印、蝕刻及鍍膜等製程，目前擁有多個國際大廠
專案。預期 AR/VR軟硬體需至 2027至 2028年才會成熟發酵。 
• 雷射封裝：為近期資本支出重點方向，主力產品為 CW Laser CoC
以及未來的 VCSEL VOD。

個股摘要

2026 /11/10

2

• 光通訊元件：發展被動光學元件，不排除整合 FAU、Metalens及上下游模零組件整合。 
• Micro OLED / Micro LED：Micro OLED在顯示器應用發展符合預期，並積極研發 Micro 
LED在光通訊領域的應用。

Q&A內容摘要 
1. Q：新世代半導體光學產品的資本支出目前的廠區規劃及進度為何？後續大客戶的營
收比重是否會下降？  
A：公告約 80億元的資本支出中，雷射封裝相關預計佔 40至 50億元，約三分之一投入台
灣廠區，其餘投入海外越南新廠。剩下的資本支出將用於 Micro LED、AR/VR波導及大客
戶新產品（廠區分散於大陸及台灣）。與大陸大客戶合作近 20年互信基礎深厚，只要有觸
控與顯示的新產品皆會參與，大客戶仍是重要客戶，但隨新業務逐步發酵，其營收佔比的
確會漸漸下降。

2. Q：10月份營收跳升破百億元的原因？這是一次性的嗎？這是否會佔用過多營運資
金？  
A：10月營收達 130億元，主因為客戶委託進行手機關鍵零組件的加工與精密對位貼合，
這是相對長期的專案，並非一次性。營收跳升主力來自高單價材料，若純看加工費，實際
營收佔比並不高。材料確實會佔用較大營運資金，但因週期短，銀行額度支持無虞；先前
提出的增資案主要是為支撐長期營運資金及較大資本支出。

3. Q：雷射封裝產線何時建好並貢獻營收？GIS在此領域相對於現有供應商的優勢在
哪？  
A：產線今年 2Q26已陸續建置，4Q26客戶原材料供應及認證趨於成熟，量產預計在 11月
或 12月從小量往上衝。GIS的優勢在於：(1)長期與國際大廠合作累積的製造管理能力。(2)
擁有充足現金流與財務實力，能支撐數十億的資本支出。

### Page 3

【中信投顧投資分析報告】 
個股摘要

2026 /11/10

3 
4. Q：雷射出貨的規格？今明兩年的資本支出預估？  
A：目前專注於大於 300mW、400mW的高功率雷射（不涉獵傳統 100mW），主要應用於
ELSFP等 200G以上高階領域，材料主力為磷化銦，同時也開發 CW Laser及 VCSEL封裝。
今年已核准資本支出約 80億元，明年資本支出將視新產品狀況及客戶需求而定，預計今年
底或明年初會較明確。

5. Q：明年筆電與平板的記憶體缺貨狀況會好轉嗎？  
A：據業界交流，因新產能開出尚需時間，記憶體缺貨狀況到 2027年底前改善幅度可能有
限，許多人預估缺貨可能持續到 2028年。

6. Q：FAU的佈局？與集團內的訊芯有無合作？  
A：集團內公司皆獨立營運，訊芯比較接近光模塊加工。GIS初期不會聚焦整套 FAU模組
組裝，而是先從 passive component（如 Fiber holder、V型槽、透鏡陣列、超透鏡）做起，
未來若元件做得不錯，才會考慮後段的模組。

7. Q：大客戶即將推出搭載 OLED及觸控功能的筆電，貴司是否有參與？ A：不便針對
單一客戶產品發表評論。但只要是分離式的觸控模組（非內嵌式 In-cell Touch），公司都會
積極爭取並參與。

8. Q：雷射封裝做到 300mW以上，在技術或設備上有何門檻與優勢？ A：100mW提升
到 300mW或 400mW，封裝的製程流程基本相同，但測試項目及設備必須大幅更新，這也
是新資本支出的重點。基於過去在顯示器背光模組及指紋辨識貼合所累積的光學製程經
驗，跨入此領域對公司來說並非太難。

### Page 4

【中信投顧投資分析報告】 
個股摘要

2026 /11/10

4 
9. Q：設備交期似乎很長，何時能滿產？ 
A：固晶機、打線機是泛用的，依照現有機台去挪動不難，主要是測試設備（如測試設備
外掛的電流計、頻譜分析儀）交期確實較長，預計 2Q27季底到 3Q27初產能可完全開出。
2Q27量產時預計會有一到兩家客戶。

10. Q：滿產能時的營收貢獻預估？何時能回收資本支出？  
A：雷射封裝商業模式以加工費為主，營收絕對金額佔比看似較小，但扣除材料費後，純
加工毛利率遠高於傳統觸控顯示業務。董事會對資本支出有嚴格的財務紀律，原則上必須
在少數幾年內完成回收。

11. Q：到 2H27可能 300mW以上的雷射市場變成熟，公司屆時產能到位可以拿下多少市
佔? 
A：公司有接觸多位 high power的客戶，每個客戶需求都很可觀，即便現在因為選舉政治
議題等潛在變數可能改變 AI的需求，但以整個產業來看，公司建置的產能僅占需求面的
一部分比例，仍有很大的增加產能的空間。

12. Q：與富采的 Micro LED及光通訊發展有何競爭或關聯？  
A：富采沒有做到封裝，GIS做封裝，雙方可能為上下游關係。富采 Micro LED偏向 TV巨
量轉移應用，GIS則聚焦 AR投影機光機應用，兩者 Micro LED大小差異很大。公司也與
客戶在探討 Micro LED或 Micro VCSEL在光通訊領域的潛力，均處於樣品開發階段。另外
公司今年 4Q26或 1Q27 AR projector可能會有小量出貨。

13. Q：光通訊相關的研發專案進度？以及信賴性測試（如 5000小時）的狀況？FAU會朝
模組發展嗎？  
A：不便針對單一客戶及具體的信賴性測試時數評論。關於 FAU，初期聚焦於光學元件（如
Fiber holder、波導、V型槽、超透鏡）。公司已具備 Metalens自行設計及製造的能力，因
其製程與目前開發的 AR眼鏡波導製程十分相近。

## Document 380: STABLECOINS_ What next for crypto assets_ (2026-10-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/STABLECOINS_ What next for crypto assets_ (2026-10-20).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank
Research

### Page 2

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?
Institutional adoption of stablecoins continues to incrementally progress in 2026. While
the frenzy from GENIUS has diminished, stablecoins’ links to the financial system have
continued to deepen, and more policymakers are seriously considering their use cases.
The market cap has hovered around ~$300bn over the past 12 months, a sign that the

stablecoin market is maturing. Stablecoin payments, while still marginal relative to
stablecoins' crypto-native purpose, are also growing, rising from around $103bn in
adjusted transaction volume in 2022 to $375bn in 2026 YTD.
For now, institutional adoption remains concentrated in cross-border payments, treasury
management, 24/7 liquidity provision, collateral, and real-time settlement.
The next big question is whether stablecoins will evolve to become merely a payments

technology, or a genuine disruptor to the global banking system. As we previously
established, the US promotion of stablecoins remains a geopolitical tool to ensure USD
dominance. Bloomberg also reported that the Trump administration is considering joint
ventures with private actors to promote USD stablecoins overseas, in a bid to boost the
dollar’s dominance and source demand for US Treasuries. Last week, Circle announced
plans to expand into 40 new markets in Southeast Asia, LatAm and the Middle East. 99%

of stablecoins (by market cap) remain pegged to USD and are largely backed by US
Treasuries.
Looking ahead, the key debate to watch is whether US regulations will allow for yield-
bearing stablecoins. Research shows that consumers say policymakers should prohibit
crypto companies from offering interest-like rewards for holding stablecoins, which will
threaten to siphon off the bank deposits that drive local lending. The US CLARITY Act
failed in Sep after lawmakers failed to agree on whether the broader crypto market

structure would support such yield-bearing stablecoins.
In this chartbook, we revisit the stablecoin market a year after the GENIUS Act was
signed. We look at: (i) how adoption has evolved, (ii) the emergence of stablecoins as
financial market plumbing via liquidity provisioning and collateral management, and (iii)
the key regulatory debates that will determine the industry’s next direction.
Payment volume has a +66% CAGR

### Page 3

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
REFRESHER: What are stablecoins?
– Definition: Digital tokens pegged to “stable” assets (e.g. USD or gold), issued
and transferred on a blockchain, which records ownership and transactions.
– Fiat-backed stablecoins are considered fully “collateralized”: USD
stablecoin with 1mn coins in circulation would be backed by $1mn in
cash and equivalents held in reserve by the stablecoin issuer.

– Types: Fiat-, commodity-, crypto- and algorithmic-backed.
– ~99% of stablecoins market valuation is pegged to the USD.
– Market cap: $5bn (2020) to ~$310bn (2026)
– Concentrated in USDT (~60%) and USDC (~23%).
– Benefits: 24/7 transfers & settlement, lower financial intermediaries,
programmability (e.g. escrow, conditional release, automated reconciliation),
and dollar-based store of value.
– Use cases:*
– Crypto trading (~77%) remains dominant.

– Payments (5-12%) growing, cross-border transfers and remittances.
– Store of value (17-33%) especially in emerging markets.
– Other: (~2%-8%): on/off ramps and tokenized RWA settlement.
– Alternatives: CBDCs, tokenized deposits, traditional financial architecture
*Estimates on market share taken from BCG analysis.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg Finance LP. Shows stablecoin total
market cap. Updated October 19 2026.
Stablecoins’ reached a plateau of ~$310bn in October 2026

### Page 4

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
ADOPTION OVERVIEW: Most stablecoin volume is still not driven by real economic
activity
Stablecoin transaction volume:
– ~$100trn (unadjusted), ~$2.42trn (adjusted) in 2026 YTD, according to
Allium.
– Chainalysis places more bullish estimates of $28trn in 2025; projected to
reach $719trn by 2035 on organic growth alone.
Stablecoin payments:
– Stablecoin payment volume grew from $151bn in 2022 to $375bn 2026 YTD

(according to VisaOnChain Analytics).
– Businesses account for the majority (58%-64%) of payments, led by B2B
payment volumes.
– Allium Sep report showed largest business payment categories include
service fees ($56bn), payroll ($43bn) and service payments ($32bn).
– Case study: Payments volume across Visa’s >160 stablecoin-linked card
programs across consumer, business and commercial activity grew ~200%
y/y.
Strongest adoption in remittances, B2B payments and dollar access in countries

with unstable currencies or weak banking systems.
– Benefits for cross-border use cases: Instant settlement, <$0.10 per
transaction, borderless, transparent, 24/7, no intermediaries. Remittance fees
are lower (0.5-3.0%) than the global fee average (~6.6%).
– Stablecoins represented 0.31% of fiat cross-border payment value in 2025.
Marginal portion but registered +64% growth increase vs fiat (+9%) according
to Allium. B2B payments had the highest cross-border share (43%).

– A 2025 IMF study also shows transaction flows reached  ~7.7% of GDP in
Latin America and ~6.7% in Africa and the Middle East.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Allium. Shows top 8 countries by received payments from Jan to August 2026.
Only the receiving end is attributed, so a $100 payment counts once, not twice. That attribution covers 13.5% of
stablecoin payments.
Stablecoins payment use case is steadily growing

### Page 5

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
CONSUMER/RETAIL PAYMENT: Still on the periphery
Consumer payments: Not yet there
– Retail adoption is still the most distant use case, especially amongst developed-market consumer credit card spend. A recent ABA survey found that only
24% of consumers think that stablecoins could provide meaningful benefits.
– Stablecoins still look more like a debit instrument than a credit product. They do not naturally replicate the consumer value proposition of credit cards (i.e.

rewards, revolving credit, dispute rights, chargebacks, fraud protection and convenience), so there is limited incentive for developed-market consumers to
use them.
– Limited scope for payments also evident in wallets (see chart below). Very little of the stablecoin supply is being held as balances in consumer wallets.
Still, the number of retail payments is incrementally growing, with cross-border, emerging market use cases strongest
– Retail transaction volume: $2.6bn (2020) to $71bn (2024); $66bn YTD in 2026.*

– Strongest use in: (i) economies with hyperinflation; (ii) poor banking access; or (iii) high remittance fees.
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Allium data.  Shows circulating supply by holder type (Sep 2026). Categories calculated as a percentage of total circulating supply. Note that wallets account for money being held vs
payments (money moving). Business wallets in this case refer to those holding smaller balances with quick turnover (i.e. payment services, merchants, gaming), while institutional wallets hold larger balances and turn them

over quickly, i.e. financial-services firms, treasuries, and wallets. Note: *Retail sized transactions are defined as transactions of $250 or less, according to Visa OnChain Analytics.
The amount of stablecoin supply in consumer wallets stands at ~14%

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
CASH/LIQUDITY MANAGEMENT: Stablecoins are evolving as a major tokenized
contender
Sources: Deutsche Bank Research, taken from DB’s Tokenised money and the future of FX and Stablecoins and
agentic commerce: new rails, old frictions.
Stablecoins can help treasury management and settlement
– Stablecoins have a strong use case for settling transactions for FX,

treasury operations and collateral management i.e. intra-day repos.
– This is because stablecoins’ 24/7 settlement can address
operational frictions in cross-border transactions.
Poses significant risk to cross-border B2B transactions for banks
– Compared to traditional banks, stablecoins offer substantial relief
from transaction costs. Our equity analysts estimate total customer
processing fee savings to increase from 0 to ~$70bn in 2035, if
consumers switch from traditional banks to stablecoins.

Stablecoins is not a replacement solution to FX settlement risk
– FX settlement requires two currencies to be exchanged
simultaneously. Currently, stablecoins only serve as an intermediary
between the two currency legs, resulting in a “stablecoin sandwich”.
– Overwhelming concentration of USD-stablecoins also limits
stablecoin ability to support widespread payment-versus payment
settlement across currency pairs.
– Stablecoins also face fragmented interoperability/liquidity issues

and regulatory uncertainty across issuers/networks/jurisdictions.
– For more, read our FX report here.
Should we explain how settlement work as it is very
interesting and Rohini has a nice chart on this ?
Probably more relevant than having the stablecoin
market cap here I would say.
A more integrated post-trade process

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
INSTITUTIONAL ADOPTION: Corporates, card networks, and banks are integrating
stablecoins
Institutions are racing to capture stablecoin market share
– Card networks: Mastercard expanded its settlement capabilities to include
stablecoins, using USDC and Paxos-issued stablecoins.
– Institutions: Exploring stablecoins via developing in-house permissioned

blockchain experiments and/or exploring issuance of proprietary stablecoins.
– >140 companies and financial institutions helped launch Open USD
(OUSD) in Sep 2026. OUSD reached $799mn of supply in 2 weeks.
Signals shift toward consortium-backed/network-led digital dollar
models.
Are stablecoins posing a threat to traditional payment systems?
– Bullish: Stablecoins to surpass traditional rails by the 2030s (Chainalysis).
– ~17% of stablecoin-linked card volume is occurring across Visa’s

business and commercial card programs. Shows growing adoption of
stablecoins for settlement, treasury management and cross-border
commerce.
– Key concerns for banks: Fee pressure from stablecoin adoption →
bank outflows → higher reliance on wholesale funding → pressure on
net interest margins.
– Base case: However, Deutsche Bank equity analysts see more limited near-
term risk. Visa and Mastercard to remain dominant especially in advanced
markets.

– KYC, AML, sanctions screening, wallet monitoring, fraud controls and
jurisdiction-specific limits remain essential for banks.
Stablecoin transactions could match Visa and Mastercard transactions
sometime between 2031 and 2039 (Chainalysis).
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Chainalysis.
Shows projected years until crypto merchant transactions reach Visa scale.

### Page 9

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
GEOPOLITICS: Why stablecoins are a "Genius" idea for the US
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, US Treasury, Tether July 2026 report. Shows Major foreign holders of Treasury Securities.
Treasury data from June 2026.
Can sustain USD dominance
– 99% of stablecoins (by market cap) are USD-pegged.
– Stablecoins function primarily as a digital version of dollars

moving over blockchain rails, extending the dollar’s reach into
new digital environments.
– Reinforces dollar savings: The more firms invoice and transact in USD,
the more they save in USD assets. Stablecoins help preserve this
feedback loop by keeping businesses and individuals in dollar
payment rails.
Can support US debt, given share held by foreigners has fallen from
>50% in early 2000s to ~30% in early 2026.
– Stablecoin issuers total holdings: ~$170bn according to Allium x

Citizen.
– Tether: ~$141bn in direct and indirect exposure to US T-bills.
– Impact: BIS shows how $3.5bn inflow into dollar-backed stablecoins
lowers 3m T-bill yields by 2.5-3.5bp under normal conditions. Effect
more than doubles in periods of T-bill shortages.
– Forecasts: T-bills holding by stablecoin issuers could reach ~$1trn by
2028 (Treasury forecasts).
– Recent Fed SF research places it closer to $400bn by the end
of 2030, lower than the US government’s financing needs.

Stablecoin issuers’  demand for US debt could counterweight declining demand from China
Tether competes with Saudi Arabia and South Korea as major holders of US Treasuries
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco, US Treasury Data. Shows Treasury
holdings, stablecoin issuers vs China.

### Page 10

Marion Laboure | Camilla Siazon |  STABLECOINS: What next for crypto assets?  |  October  20, 2026
US GENIUS ACT: Debate on rules around payment stablecoins one year on
GENIUS provisions
– Strict issuer requirements: Issuers can be private, non-bank entity, but must
meet strict federal or state requirements.
– Reserves: Requires 1:1 backing with US cash, 93 days T-bills, repos/reverse
repos, or money market funds holding those assets.
– AML/KYC/BSA compliance obligations to stablecoin issuers, aligning them

exams, and strong internal controls.
Next steps
– GENIUS takes effect no later than 18 Jan 2027, or 120 days after final
implementing regulations are issued.
– Ongoing pushback from US banks as to whether GENIUS ban on yield-
bearing stablecoins should extend to affiliate or third-party arrangements,
given many users do not hold stablecoins directly with the issuer.
– CLARITY ACT failed in Sep because of disagreements on how
exchanges/broader crypto market structure should handle stablecoins.

– The US Treasury has proposed implementing rules for payment stablecoins.
There are two ways stablecoin yield can be paid; ongoing debate over the three-
party model
Source: Deutsche Bank Research, Congressional Research Service.

RISKS: Still hotly discussed amongst policymakers and financial institutions
Bank liquidity risk
Loss of stable funding source for banks from deposit outflow (not offset by
partial flowback from stablecoin issuers’ reserve deposit)
System stability risk
Stablecoins can fail to maintain their $1 value during times of stress. Evidence shows
that USDC and Tether actually trade within 1% of their dollar value but prices can

diverge sharply during stress episodes.
Run on stablecoin issuer(s) in market stress / trust scenario triggering fire sales of
reserve assets and/or deposit withdrawals.

❑ For USD stablecoins like

stablecoins, e.g. gold with only
small market share

US

EU
Markets in Crypto-Asset Regulation
(MiCAR)
Backing Assets*
• 100% backing assets in USD cash or short-term
Treasuries (≤93-day T-bills).
• Non-significant EMTs –At least 30% reserves in
same-currency bank deposits, remainder in HQLA

• Significant EMTs - At least 60% of the reserves in
bank deposits; remainder in HQLA
• Systemic stablecoins – 30% unremunerated BoE
deposits vs 70% in UK sovereign debt (HQLA)
• Non-systemic – Full 1:1 backing in HQLR assets,
minimum 5% in on demand deposits.
UK
Multi-issuance
Stance
• Rules are still being fine-tuned; no specific focus
yet on multi-issuance.
• Multi-issuance under active review; EC considers multi-
issuance permissible, an equivalence-based framework for

EU/third-country arrangements is under legislative
consideration.
• BoE considers multi-issuance unsuitable for systemic
UK use. Preference for single-issuance models backed
by single reserve (incl. Non-GPB denom. stablecoins) .
UK Cryptoasset Regime (FCA)
UK Systemic Stablecoins Regime (BoE)
Eligible Issuers
• Bank subsidiaries, federal nonbank licensees, or
state issuers with federal registration, with
enhanced oversight for >$10bn circulation.

• EMTs: EU-authorised credit institutions or authorised
electronic money institutions. Restrictions apply to
non-EU currency EMTs (exceeding MiCAR
thresholds) as a means of exchange
• FCA-authorised firms for non-systemic stablecoins,
• Systemic issuers to be designated by HM Treasury
and jointly regulated under BoE/FCA oversight
Balance Sheet
Treatment
• Stablecoins treated as reserve-backed digital
payment assets, segregated in bankruptcy-remote

accounts and treated as cash equivalents
• EMTs are fiat-referenced cryptoassets subject to
MiCAR and e-money requirements
• (OCC/FDIC) Mandates prudential capital buffers
tailored to issuer risk profile.
• (Fed) Framework consistent with bank requirements
• EMI issuers: minimum own funds of 2% of EMT
supply; 3% for significant EMTs. Significant issuers
subject to enhanced risk-management requirements
• Systemic: 6 months operating expenses/largest

plausible loss event
• Non-systemic: 1% av. stablecoins in circulation
Capital Reserve
Requirements**

AUTHORS
Marion Laboure is a Managing Director and Macro Strategist at Deutsche Bank in London, and a Lecturer in Economics and Finance at
Harvard University.
She was previously an economist at the IMF, the Central Bank of Luxembourg, Barclays, and the European Commission. She has twice
received the First Prize of the American Society of Actuaries, was named a rising star in finance by Revue Banque, and is recognised

among the 45 standout women in fintech globally.
She holds a Bachelor's degree in Mathematics from the University of Paris Dauphine, a Master of Research in Economics from the
London School of Economics, and a PhD in Econometrics and Quantitative Economics from the École Normale Supérieure.
She is the author of Democratizing Finance published by Harvard University Press.
Camilla Siazon is an Associate on Deutsche Bank’s Macro and Thematic team in London.

She focuses on digital assets, geopolitics, and global macroeconomic trends. Prior to Deutsche Bank, she worked at geopolitical
advisory firms Eurasia Group and Greenmantle.
She holds a dual MA/MSc in International History from Columbia University and the London School of Economics, and a BA in History
from Barnard College, Columbia University.

^New 
Transitioning to mid-cycle 
• We now favour a more balanced stance between stocks and bonds. Near-
term event risks and high earnings expectations prompt us to trim equities to a 
Core holding. The US and Asia ex-Japan (AxJ) remain our preferred regions. 
Within AxJ, we upgrade India to Overweight. 
• Higher bond yields are improving the outlook for future returns and offering 
increasingly attractive income opportunities. We are Overweight Emerging 
Market (EM) USD bonds and favour corporate over government bonds, but limit 
duration to 3-7 years. 
• We remain Overweight gold. Prices are likely to grind higher as near-term 
headwinds from higher bond yields are offset by EM central bank demand. 
Great expectations 
Individual risk events, high earnings expectations and a higher cost of capital 
point to a more moderate return environment ahead. In the very near term, 
financial markets must navigate key event risks, such as (i) the US mid-term elections, 
which have historically been associated with elevated market volatility and (ii) renewed 
geopolitical tensions, with several reports suggesting the US is considering renewed 
strikes on Iran. These concerns have pushed the Brent oil price well above USD 
110/bbl despite significant ongoing crude oil flows through the Strait of Hormuz.  
Higher bond yields combined with high earnings expectations raise the risk of 
an expectations reset. The US Q3 earnings season is attracting significant attention 
given high earnings expectations, particularly for the AI sector. However, higher US 
bond yields may start to bite as debt-financed capex takes on a greater share of total 
capex over the coming year. This, combined with already-high expectations, raises the 
risk of disappointment and a near-term repricing of expected earnings growth pace.  
Despite the current challenges, healthy growth continues to underpin the 
economic backdrop. The US economic outlook remains well supported, and the 
absolute level of year-ahead earnings expectations is high. We do not expect Fed rate 
hikes to result in overly restrictive policy. Our caution, thus, stems from the risk of an 
expectations repricing rather than an outright economic slowdown.

Global Market Outlook 4

Fig. 1 Our quant model score has fallen to levels 
associated with flat-to-modestly-negative equity returns  
 Fig. 2 Negative gold-USD correlation has held back

Our quantitative stock-bond model  Gold and USD correlations over the last 15 years

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered  Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered

A more balanced stock-bond mix 
We trim global equities to a Core holding, leaving us 
Neutral on both equities and bonds and Overweight gold. 
The concerns laid out earlier point to near-term volatility risks 
and a somewhat lower long-term growth trajectory for 
equities. Our quant model also reflects this cautious outlook, 
having downgraded equities to a level usually associated with 
flat-to-modestly-negative returns. 
From a relative perspective, though, the higher yields on 
offer from bonds also raise the bar by presenting an 
increasingly compelling return outlook after several 
years of very low yields. We believe this argues for portfolios 
that are increasingly balanced between equities and bonds. 
Ways to diversify equity exposure 
We continue to favour global equity market 
diversification, with a preference for US and AxJ equities. 
We remain Overweight US and AxJ equities, expecting them 
to outperform other major regions. Earnings growth remains 
the main driver of this view – while growth rates could 
decelerate somewhat, we still expect them to remain higher 
than those of other major regions.  
Japan and Euro area equities remain attractive as 
regional diversifiers. Our positive view on both markets’ 
financial sectors supports our Core holding view on them.   
Within Asia, we are Overweight Taiwan and India. Indian 
equities have lagged the region this year, in part due to limited 
listed AI-related exposure. However, we believe this is 
increasingly likely to become an advantage as investors seek 
greater diversification away from excessive concentration in 
the AI theme. This comes at a time when the economic and 
earnings growth outlook for Indian equities is starting to 
improve. We retain a more Neutral view on Chinese equities 
amid a still-challenging growth outlook. 
We also favour greater sector diversification. In the US, 
we favour diversifying across our Overweight sectors of 
technology, financials, materials and healthcare. We also 
favour financials in Japan and the Euro area.  
Changes to our equity opportunistic ideas also reflect a 
more defensive stance. In recent weeks, we have closed our 
global semiconductors, MSCI Taiwan, US communication 
services and global power & electrification ideas and have 
opened new ideas on US healthcare and MSCI Switzerland. 
Riding the yield train 
We believe bond markets offer an attractive opportunity 
to lock in yields. The 10-year US government bond yield is 
now in a range last witnessed in 2007. While the process of 
rising yields results in lower bond prices – as painfully 
experienced in recent months – the yield level itself starts to 
significantly improve the outlook for future returns. 
Favour corporate over government bonds, but check 
your duration. Still-high credit quality means that we continue 
to favour taking on credit risk to earn a higher yield (EM USD 
bonds are preferred, but we also see investment-grade (IG) 
and high-yield (HY) bonds as Core holdings). However, 
limiting duration to 3-7 years remains key to avoiding 
excessive exposure to any inflation surprises. 
Changes to our bond opportunistic ideas also reflect a 
more balanced stance. We close our EU bank contingent 
convertible bonds (CoCos2 – please refer page 29) and US 
utilities corporate hybrids ideas. We also close our 
opportunistic idea on US 10-year government bonds following 
the recent pullback in yields, locking in gains. 
Alternatives and gold to remain relevant 
Alternatives are an effective tool for portfolio risk 
management. Hedge funds and private markets provide a 
differentiated return stream, while rotating into alternatives 
helps reduce equity beta exposure. We examine downside 
capture and how beta is reduced via hedge fund sub-
strategies and private markets in our asset allocation section.  
Gold remains a key Overweight. We trim our three- and 12-
month forecasts modestly, but see gold forming a strong base 
above USD 4,000/oz. Rising bond yields and the USD were 
recent headwinds, but we expect a softer USD and continued 
EM central bank demand to result in 12-month gains.

The Foundation and Foundation+ models are allocations that you can use as the starting point for building a diversified 
investment portfolio. The Foundation model showcases a set of allocations focusing on traditional asset classes that are 
accessible to most investors, while the Foundation+ model includes allocations to private assets that may be accessible to 
investors in some jurisdictions, but not others.

2 Foundation: Our tactical asset allocation

### Page 7

Global Market Outlook 7

Summary

Artificial intelligence (AI) has become one of the most powerful drivers of global market performance and, increasingly, 
one of the largest contributors to portfolio risk. While we remain constructive on the long-term outlook for equities and 
continue to view AI as a transformational investment theme, our tactical positioning has recently shifted from an Overweight to 
a Neutral equity weight. This reflects a more balanced near-term risk-reward profile after a period of strong equity market 
performance and increasingly concentrated market leadership. 
Against this backdrop, a deeper understanding of portfolio construction is essential. Investors should consider not only 
how much they allocate to equities, bonds and alternative investments, but also the underlying factors driving portfolio risk. Using 
a Total Portfolio Approach (TPA), we assessed the factor exposures embedded within our Foundation and Foundation+ 
Balanced portfolios, with a particular focus on AI concentration risk. 
Our analysis suggests both portfolios are more diversified than a traditional 60/40 allocation and maintain meaningful 
exposure to long-term AI growth opportunities. The Foundation+ Balanced portfolio provides additional diversification 
through alternative investments and a broader range of return drivers, reducing reliance on public equity markets.

Key findings

Looking beyond asset classes: Understanding 
AI concentration risk 
Investors often assess diversification through the lens of asset 
allocation. A portfolio containing equities, bonds, gold and 
alternatives may appear diversified on the surface. However, 
this traditional approach alone does not always reveal where 
portfolio risks are truly concentrated. 
TPA starts from a different premise. Rather than focusing solely 
on asset classes, it seeks to identify the underlying factors that 
drive portfolio outcomes, including equity markets, interest 
rates, credit spreads, currencies, liquidity conditions and 
thematic exposures such as AI. 
This perspective is particularly relevant following our recent 
move from an Overweight to a Neutral equity allocation. While 
we remain constructive on equities over the long term, a 
growing share of market returns has been driven by a relatively 
narrow group of AI-related beneficiaries. For investors, the 
question is, therefore, not whether to maintain exposure to AI, 
but whether their portfolios have become overly dependent on 
a single theme or underlying risk factor.

### Page 8

Global Market Outlook 8

US equities exhibit some of the highest levels of AI intensity, 
reflecting the dominant role US companies play across the AI 
value chain. By contrast, fixed income, private assets and gold 
display significantly lower AI sensitivity and derive their 
returns from different economic drivers. 
The objective is not to eliminate AI exposure. We continue to 
believe AI will remain one of the defining structural growth 
themes of the coming decade. Instead, the objective is to 
ensure portfolios participate in this opportunity without 
becoming excessively dependent on it. 
Stronger diversification through different 
return drivers 
One of the most common misconceptions is that greater 
diversification inevitably leads to lower returns. Our analysis 
suggests the opposite may be true when diversification is 
achieved through genuinely different return drivers. Both 
Foundation and Foundation + portfolios exhibit attractive risk-
return characteristics relative to a traditional 60/40 allocation 
(see Fig. 9). They deliver comparable or higher return 
potential with lower volatility, smaller drawdowns and reduced 
sensitivity to global equity markets.

The key insight is that diversification works best when risks 
originate from different economic drivers. Simply increasing 
the number of securities or asset classes is not sufficient. 
What matters is the breadth of return sources supporting 
portfolio outcomes. 
AI remains a meaningful component of portfolio risk, and its 
exposure is spread across several parts of the ecosystem. 
Non-AI-related factors, including equity beta, credit, 
currencies and liquidity premia, also drive portfolio risk. This 
broader mix reduces dependence on any single investment 
theme and creates a more balanced portfolio structure. 
The role of alternatives: Creating additional 
return engines 
The most important distinction between our Foundation and 
Foundation+ portfolios is the inclusion of hedge funds and 
private assets in the latter. Alternatives can provide additional 
return engines and reduce reliance on equity market beta, but 
different strategies offer very different forms of diversification. 
Within hedge funds, a multi-strategy provides a strong 
starting point for investors seeking lower equity sensitivity and 
differentiated return sources, with an equity beta of around 
0.15-0.20 and a correlation slightly above 50% to global 
equities’ performance. Equity long/short retains greater 
participation in equity-led growth, reflected in a higher 0.45 
beta, and has historically captured 56% of global equity gains. 
However, it also maintains greater common exposure to 
equity and AI factors. Relative value offers the lowest 
equity exposure, with a beta of around 0.10 and participation 
below 5% of global equity losses, although nearly 80% of its 
modelled risk is attributed to credit (partly reflecting the use of 
convertible-arbitrage trades), highlighting that reducing equity 
beta can mean substituting it with another source of risk.  
Within private markets, the sources of return also differ 
materially. Private credit offers the most differentiated 
return engine, with returns driven primarily by income and 
credit premia rather than equity appreciation. Private equity, 
by contrast, remains more closely linked to equity and 
growth factors, making it a stronger source of long-term return 
potential but a less effective tool for reducing portfolio beta. 
Infrastructure can provide an attractive middle ground. It 
retains exposure to AI-related structural themes, particularly 
through digital and power infrastructure, and offers lower equity 
beta and correlation than traditional public equities. 
Ultimately, our move from an Overweight to a Core allocation 
in equity markets does not change our constructive long-term 
view on equities or AI. Rather, it reinforces the importance of 
understanding where portfolio risk originates. Investors do not 
need to choose between participating in the AI opportunity 
and maintaining diversification. Using a TPA lens combines 
exposure to long-term growth themes with a broader set of 
return drivers, including carefully selected alternatives, and 
reduces dependence on any single source of market 
performance.

### Page 9

Global Market Outlook 9

Key themes

Core scenario (soft landing, 45% probability): An economic soft landing remains our core scenario, with the global economy 
overcoming this year’s oil price shock thanks to a surge in AI investment in the US and Asia. Record wealth generation driven 
by the stock market rally is supporting US consumption. Fiscal easing in the US, Germany and Japan is supporting growth. 
Meanwhile, inflation has reaccelerated due to a rebound in fuel prices as the Middle East conflict drags on. Against this backdrop, 
the Fed, ECB and BoJ all hiked rates in the same month (in September) for the first time. Although all three subsequently paused 
in October, we expect the Fed to hike twice more, the ECB once more and the BoJ thrice more by June 2027. Nevertheless, we 
expect the energy and tariff effects on inflation to fade by H2 2027, enabling the Fed and the ECB to cut rates by Q3 2027. 
Meanwhile, China is likely to target credit easing and accelerate fiscal spending in H1 2027 to revive domestic growth.  
Upside risk (no landing, 30% probability): We maintain the probability at 30% as AI-driven investment growth continues. 
There is a reasonable chance that lower oil prices next year, the AI-fuelled wealth boom and fiscal easing in Germany and Japan 
will boost ‘animal spirits’. A potential global defence spending boom, led by the US and Europe, could also lift global growth. 
Downside risk (25% probability): We raised the risk of a recession to 15%, especially if central banks are forced to accelerate 
the pace of rate hikes to douse rising inflation. A stock market downturn negating the wealth effect or a bond sell-off due to fiscal 
concerns are other tail risks. We cut stagflation probability to 10% as we see low risk of oil prices sustaining above USD 120/bbl.

Policy rates watch  
We expect the Fed to hike twice more by Q2 2027, then 
ease in H2 2027, as AI-led expansion continues. The US 
economy remains firmly in expansion. In Q3, annualised GDP 
growth accelerated to 3.4%, led by AI investments and 
consumption. In October, the composite Purchasing 
Managers’ Index (PMI) remained strong at 57.6. Since our last 
monthly report, the consensus estimate for 2026 GDP growth 
has been nudged up to 2.3% from 2.1%, and the 2027 
estimate has risen to 2.2%. That matches the Fed’s upgraded 
growth projection of 2.3% for 2026, but remains below the 
Fed’s 2.4% estimate for 2027. AI investment, fiscal support 
and household wealth are the main growth drivers, although 
consumer activity lags business spending. Higher bond yields 
and elevated oil prices are headwinds, but recession risks 
look contained. Our core scenario remains a ‘soft landing’, at 
45%, with a meaningful 30% chance of a ‘no-landing’ upside. 
Rising gasoline and diesel prices pushed US core personal 
consumption expenditures (PCE) inflation to 0.3% m/m and 
3.1% y/y, matching estimates. Consensus sees core PCE 
inflation slowing from 3.2% in Q4 2026 to 2.4% by Q4 2027 
as energy and tariff effects fade. Underlying price momentum 
is softening, and recent PCE methodology revisions have 
lowered core inflation. However, fuel costs passed on to wider 
consumers and AI-driven chip shortages remain upside risks. 
In October, the Fed held rates at 4.0%, after hiking in 
September for the first time since 2023. We expect the Fed to 
hike rates by 25bps in December and 25bps more in H1 2027 
due to elevated near-term energy inflation. It is likely to follow 
with a 25bps cut to 4.25% in Q3 2027 as disinflation returns. 
This aligns with the Fed’s projected policy path, although it 
implies a more dovish trajectory than money markets, which 
are pricing another 56bps of hikes over the next 12 months. 
Political uncertainty after the mid-term election is a key risk.

6 Macro overview – at a glance

Rajat Bhattacharya 
Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 10

Global Market Outlook 10 
ECB to hike once more in December, then pause. The 
Euro area recovery is firming. GDP grew 1.2% y/y in Q2 2026, 
up from 0.6% in Q1, and the manufacturing PMI stayed robust 
at 52.4 in October. Consensus 2026 growth has been raised 
to 1.0% from 0.9%, and 2027 is unchanged at 1.3%. The main 
supports are resilient exports and German infrastructure and 
defence spending. However, cautious consumers and soft 
private investment limit the recovery, and energy costs are 
squeezing household incomes and business margins. 
Recession is not the central expectation. French fiscal stress 
and sustained high energy costs remain the key risks. 
Higher energy prices pushed Euro area headline inflation up 
to 3.8% y/y in September from 3.2% y/y in August, while core 
inflation rose to 2.5% y/y. Consensus expects headline and 
core inflation to ease to 2% y/y and 2.4% y/y by Q4 2027, 
respectively. Encouragingly, energy has had limited impact on 
core prices, and longer-term inflation expectations remain 
stable at around 2.1%. Together, these should allow inflation 
to moderate in 2027. The main risk is that energy, and 
especially gas, prices stay high for longer than expected. 
The ECB held its deposit rate at 2.5% in October after raising 
it by 50bps this year. Inflation data continue to favour an 
additional hike in December. We expect an ECB rate hold in 
H1 2027, followed by a cut by Q3 2027 as disinflation returns. 
This policy path is more dovish than money markets, which 
are pricing more than 60bps of hikes over the next 12 months. 
China to provide targeted support to defend growth floor. 
China's growth momentum recovered in September. Both the 
manufacturing PMI (50.1) and the non-manufacturing PMI 
(50.2) moved back into expansion. The construction PMI rose 
above 50 for the first time this year as infrastructure spending 
started to take effect. Exports grew 36.3% y/y, pushing the 
trade surplus to a record USD 133.5bn. We expect growth in 
2026 to meet the lower end of the 4.5-5.0% target. Growth is 
increasingly bifurcated, though, with technology firms and 
exporters doing well, while household demand stays weak. 
Prices are rising again, but only narrowly. Consumer inflation 
edged up to 0.9% y/y in September, and producer inflation 
surged to 5% y/y, its highest since 2022, driven by energy and 
metals prices and concentrated in upstream sectors. With 
domestic demand weak and a high comparison base next 
year, the boost from energy prices is likely to fade in 2027. 
Policy is in ‘targeted easing’ mode. We expect a modest fiscal 
package to defend the growth floor rather than a large 
stimulus. The PBoC cut a key rate by 25bps to 1.5% to fund 
‘Six Networks’ infrastructure projects and raised its relending 
quotas. A new interest subsidy on first-home mortgages 
provides limited support to demand. We expect no policy rate 
cut this year. There is still room to speed up fiscal spending, 
including by using CNY 500bn of unused local-government 
debt quota. If the PBoC switches to an overnight reverse repo 
rate as its policy rate, that would imply a de facto rate cut as 
the overnight rate is lower than the 7-day official policy rate. 
BoJ to hike at a quarterly pace until Q2 2027. Japan's 
economy remains solid. Wages are growing above 4%, and 
the labour market is tight. The main headwind is high energy 
prices, which is slowing growth. Fiscal spending gives some 
support: the FY27 draft budget includes ambitious spending 
plans, alongside a proposed two-year cut to the consumption 
tax on food and a new five-year defence programme. 
However, this extra spending raises questions about fiscal 
sustainability. The BoJ unveiled plans to triple the pace of ETF 
sales from January 2027, potentially funding the tax cut. 
Price pressures now look firmly established. Headline and 
core inflation rose above 2% for the first time this year, and 
core-core inflation (ex-fresh food and energy) jumped to 2.4% 
and is expected to rise towards 3% in Q1 2027. Breakeven 
inflation rates have stayed above 2% most of this year. Real 
interest rates remain negative, hurting savers and pensioners. 
The BoJ held its policy rate at 1.25% in October, after raising 
it in September, only three months after its previous hike. This 
points to an accelerated quarterly hiking pace. We expect the 
quarterly pace of hikes to continue, taking the rate to 2% by 
June 2027. Money markets have caught up with our hawkish 
view in recent months. The BoJ noted for the first time that 
underlying inflation has reached its 2% target, marking a shift 
from creating inflation to containing it. Politics could slow the 
hiking cycle in H2 2027. Two government-appointed board 
members voted against the September hike, and the two most 
hawkish members are due to leave in July 2027.

Global Market Outlook 11

Our view

Foundation: Bond yields remain near multi-year highs, offering an attractive entry point. We view fixed income as a Core holding 
(ie, a Neutral allocation) in our multi-asset portfolios. Within the asset class, we maintain an Overweight stance on EM USD 
government bonds, given their attractive risk-reward. We hold an Underweight view on Developed Market (DM) government 
bonds due to the tail risks associated with term premia. 
The Fed’s decision to hold rates at 3.75-4.00% in October amid still-elevated energy prices suggests that every Fed meeting 
from here is ‘live’, with a rate change possible on incoming data. With US inflation still running hot and the labour market tight, 
we expect two more 25bps hikes (one by year-end and one in H1 2027), bringing the Fed’s target range for the federal funds 
rate to 4.25-4.5%. We continue to favour the 3-7-year part of the US government bond yield curve as the best hedge against 
any risk-off move driven by AI-related growth fears. As inflation pressures ease in H2 2027, we see room for the Fed to consider 
a rate cut, which should pull nominal yields lower. Solid corporate fundamentals and supportive technicals should keep spreads 
tight, but there is limited room for them to tighten further. We still favour EM USD over local-currency (LCY) government bonds. 
Opportunistic ideas: We are bullish on US TIPS, AAA-rated CLOs, broad US HYs and Australian dollar (AUD) corporate bonds.

DM rates – Underweight 
We remain Underweight DM government bonds. In the US, 
government bond yields have retraced meaningfully after a 
sharp bear steepening through September and early October. 
We expect real yields to stay elevated over the next six 
months as AI capex and productivity growth continue to drive 
above-trend economic growth. We expect two more 25bps 
rate hikes by June 2027.  
Over the next few months, we expect US government bond 
yields to remain rangebound. The near-term path hinges on 
five factors: 1) the 3 November US mid-term election, where 
a ‘Democratic sweep’ would likely be bond-supportive by 
constraining US President Trump’s fiscal spending agenda, 
while a ‘Republican sweep’ would likely weigh on bonds as 
markets price in further fiscal expansion and a higher term 
premium; 2) Fed communication at upcoming policy meetings; 
3) the US Treasury’s quarterly refunding announcement on 4 
November, where any signal of reduced long-end issuance 
would likely compress long-end yields; 4) softening auction 
demand and repatriation by foreign investors, which add 
uncertainty around demand for US government bonds; and 5) 
the fluid geopolitical situation in the Middle East, which is 
keeping oil prices elevated, with a risk of further upside. 
Going into H2 2027, we expect moderating oil prices and the 
fading inflationary impact of tariffs to give the Fed room to 
consider rate cuts. The US 30-year yield, however, is likely to 
stay elevated as fiscal deficit remains substantial next year.  
We expect a final ECB hike to 2.75% by December 2026, then 
a pause in H1 2027, followed by a 25bps cut in Q3 2027. In 
Japan, we expect quarterly pace of BoJ hikes, with one hike 
that takes the policy rate to 1.50% by December 2026 and two 
more in H1 2027, reaching 2.00% before pausing at mid-year.

7 Fixed Income – at a glance

Jonathan Liang, CFA 
CIO, Fixed Income & FX 
Cedric Lam

Anthony Naab, CFA

### Page 12

Global Market Outlook 12 
DM corporates – Core holding 
We maintain a Neutral allocation to both DM IG and DM HY 
corporate bonds, with a tactical bias to add exposure on 
episodic weakness, as carry continues to dominate total 
returns across DM credit. Credit spreads have widened from 
their lows but remain close to historical tights, while yields are 
still attractive, reinforcing a carry-led stance. This tight-spread 
regime is anchored by robust issuer fundamentals, strong 
profitability and resilient balance sheets, despite a visible 
moderation in rating upgrades over the past quarter.  
Technical supply dynamics are reshaping sector dispersion, 
especially in the IG space, where heavy AI-related issuance 
is weighing on the long end of the curve. AI-driven supply is 
surpassing USD 250bn in 2026 and making up a growing 
share of US IG gross issuance, but there are growing signs 
that hyperscalers are turning to private markets for funding, 
which should help ease public bond market supply pressures.  
We remain selective at the long end of the yield curve and 
favour a duration of 3-7 years, where risk-adjusted carry 
remains most durable. Issuance-driven concessions in 
sectors with strong capex cycles (notably technology, capital 
goods and basic industries) present opportunities for investors 
who can stomach near-term volatility. 
Fig. 14 DM IG spreads have widened from their lows, 
but remain near historically tight levels 
Bloomberg Global Aggregate Corp Index, option-adjusted 
spread (OAS)

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
DM HY spreads have widened to roughly a six-month high. 
Persistent doubts about whether hyperscalers can generate 
adequate returns on massive AI capex investments triggered 
a broad risk asset sell-off. The CCC tier has become the focal 
point of genuine credit concern, with yields hovering near the 
high-15% range and spreads near a four-year high. Market 
focus has shifted to the funding pressures facing AI-related 
neocloud companies. We view this as idiosyncratic for now 
and see a limited impact on the broader HY universe. The 
underlying credit picture is mixed. Leverage is elevated and 
interest coverage is thin, but the broad market has not yet 
shown signs of systemic stress. The US HY default rate 
remains below trend in 2026. 
HY offers an attractive income proposition, with all-in yields of 
over 8%. Its shorter-duration profile, combined with its lower 
sensitivity to changes in the term premium and rate volatility, 
make it an attractive sleeve in the current phase of the cycle. 
We remain attentive to evolving credit rating migration trends 
and sector-specific supply pressures, particularly as upgrades 
slow and new issuance clusters around key themes. However, 
healthy issuer fundamentals and stable credit metrics 
continue to support it as a Core holding allocation. 
Fig. 15 DM HY spreads are off the tights 
Bloomberg Global HY Aggregate Corporate Index, OAS

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
EM government bonds – Overweight 
USD/Core holding for LCY 
We are Overweight EM USD government bonds and retain 
EM LCY government bonds as a Core holding, reflecting a 
balanced perspective across both regional performance and 
currency dynamics. In EM USD sovereign debt, the recent 
spread widening offered an attractive entry point to gain 
exposure to areas benefiting from resilient global growth and 
stable external conditions. Technicals remain favourable, with 
supply being front-loaded and investor positioning remaining 
relatively light compared with historical levels. 
Many EM central banks having tightened earlier and more 
aggressively post-pandemic, preserving credibility and 
maintaining positive real rates. External balances have 
improved, reserve buffers have increased and greater 
reliance on domestic funding reduces external vulnerability. 
Lula’s surprise win in Brazil’s presidential election raises 
political risk. The country’s fiscal trajectory under the incoming 
administration is a key risk to monitor. Fiscal slippage could 
trigger a broader repricing of LATAM risk. In Asia, higher-rated 
issuers continue to offer a valuable buffer during bouts of 
global rate volatility, while select frontier exposures in EMEA 
remain more sensitive to liquidity shifts and changes in risk 
appetite. Index composition also shapes resilience: the EM 
USD benchmark is less exposed to oil price shocks, as 
countries that are not oil importers account for a larger share 
of the benchmark than in the LCY benchmark. We expect 
returns to be primarily driven by carry rather than further 
spread compression.

### Page 13

Global Market Outlook 13 
Fig. 16 EM USD government bonds offer a yield pick-up 
of nearly 3ppt over DM peers 
Bloomberg EM USD Government Bond Index, yield to worst

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered 
EM LCY debt faces additional headwinds, especially as 
elevated US real yields and an appreciating USD dampen 
demand from foreign investors and reduce the comparative 
attractiveness of broad-based duration strategies. However, 
real yields remain comparatively supportive in several 
markets, and current valuations signal selective opportunities, 
especially given the restrictive monetary policy stances 
prevalent across many EM economies. Accordingly, EM LCY 
bonds retain their Core holding status, with portfolio emphasis 
placed on country selection and measured duration exposure, 
reflecting the differentiated currency dynamics at play. 
Key risks to both the USD and LCY segments include a 
potential escalation of the Middle East conflict, higher oil 
prices, renewed hawkishness from the Fed that could support 
further USD strength and weather-related disruptions, such as 
the effects of El Niño. Climate risks could prolong inflation 
pressures and hinder disinflation in vulnerable EM regions. 
Asia USD bonds – Core holding 
We retain a Neutral allocation to Asia USD bonds, an asset 
class that continues to reward investors with attractive 
nominal yields, favourable supply-demand technicals and 
robust credit fundamentals. Healthy corporate cash flows, low 
leverage and a high share of sovereign-linked issuers lend the 
segment a defensive quality relative to broader credit. 
The resilience has been evident year to date (YTD). Asia USD 
bonds have outperformed both DM government and DM IG 
corporate bonds on a total-return basis. We expect this 
relative strength to persist. The combination of carry and 
credit quality (over 60% of issues are sovereign or sovereign-
related) anchors our conviction in the asset class, even as we 
maintain a Neutral stance.

Bond opportunistic views 
Bullish US HY bonds 
We are opportunistically bullish on US HY bonds. We expect 
HY corporate earnings and cash flows to remain solid and 
leverage to remain stable. US HY bonds’ shorter-duration 
profile offers lower sensitivity to interest-rate volatility, which 
is an attractive characteristic in an environment of elevated 
term premia. 
Bullish US inflation-protected bonds (TIPS) 
Real yields remain elevated versus historical standards, 
driven by rate hike expectations. Inflation concerns persist as 
energy prices remain subject to upside risks. We believe TIPS 
offer protection against near-term inflation uncertainty.   
Bullish AUD corporate bonds 
The threshold for further RBA hikes has risen, as higher 
energy prices weigh on the growth outlook and complicate the 
policy trade-off. The transmission channel matters too, where 
higher long-end yields could feed through to the property 
market, tightening financial conditions without the RBA having 
to act, casting further doubt on the case for additional hikes. 
Technicals have improved slightly as higher yields slow new 
supply. Supply remains a tail risk, though. The AUD bond

market is much smaller than the USD market, especially at the 
long end of the curve, where demand for duration is thinner 
and the term premium is most sensitive to supply. 
Bullish AAA-rated CLOs 
Concerns about spillovers from private credit are mounting 
amid deteriorating covenant protections in parts of the 
leveraged credit market. However, we believe AAA-rated 
CLOs backed by solid asset portfolios and substantial credit 
enhancements underneath senior tranches should help 
dampen credit risk and volatility. We see a compelling case 
for floating-rate and shorter-duration alternatives as portfolio 
diversifiers amid elevated term premia.  
Close utilities corporate hybrids 
AI capex is set to keep rising, but funding it has become 
harder. Tighter financial conditions now coincide with growing 
competition for capital. Utilities' credit fundamentals remain 
strong. Even so, we close our utilities corporate hybrids idea 
to lock in a gain of 0.5% as a risk-management step.

### Page 14

Global Market Outlook 14

Our view

We downgrade global equities from Overweight to a Core allocation as we expect higher volatility in the near term. We 
expect this volatility to arise as equity market leadership broadens during the transition to a mid-cycle bull market. Equity returns 
will likely moderate from the 15-20% annualised range of the last few years but remain positive. We remain Overweight US and 
AxJ equities, which are expected to deliver the highest earnings growth in 2027 among the major regions. 
Within AxJ, we remain Overweight Taiwan, given structural AI-driven growth, and upgrade India to Overweight, for diversified 
non-AI growth exposure. South Korea is a Core holding, with attractive valuations but extreme volatility. China is also a Core 
holding, with attractive valuations but lacking a positive catalyst. We are Underweight ASEAN, which has a weaker growth profile. 
We maintain Japan as a Core holding as nominal growth supports broad-based earnings revisions. We also maintain a Core 
allocation to Europe ex-UK, which is enjoying an earnings recovery. We remain Underweight UK equities, given the market’s 
limited exposure to growth sectors.
